Full S S -Matrices and Witten Diagrams with Relative L ∞ L_{\infty} -Algebras
Luigi Alfonsi, Leron Borsten, Hyungrok Kim

Abstract
The L ∞ L_{\infty} -algebra approach to scattering amplitudes elegantly describes the nontrivial part of the S S -matrix but fails to take into account the trivial part. We argue that the trivial contribution to the S S -matrix should be accounted for by another, complementary L ∞ L_{\infty} -algebra, such that a perturbative field theory is described by a cyclic relative L ∞ L_{\infty} -algebra. We further demonstrate that this construction reproduces Witten diagrams that arise in AdS/CFT including, in particular, the trivial Witten diagrams corresponding to CFT two-point functions. We also discuss Chern–Simons theory and Yang–Mills theory on manifolds with boundaries using this approach.



School of Mathematics and Physics, University of Surrey, Guildford
gu2 7xh
, United Kingdom

l.alfonsi@herts.ac.uk
,
l.borsten@herts.ac.uk

1
Introduction and results

While Lagrangians determine tree-level scattering amplitudes, it is well known that sometimes different Lagrangians give equivalent scattering amplitudes. Put differently, Lagrangians carry redundant information. The on-shell–scattering–amplitudes programme sheds this redundancy by constructing the amplitudes directly, often removing the necessity of a Lagrangian altogether. This has led to both powerful computational tools and many new insights (see
[
1
]
and references therein for reviews).

A complementary approach is to identify a natural notion of equivalence between Lagrangians that encode the same physics. The appropriate equivalence relation, adopted here, amongst Lagrangians is given in terms of quasi-isomorphisms between the cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebras governing physical theories.

The starting point of this picture is the observation that field theories described by actions correspond to cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebras. In a nutshell, a cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebra
1
1

1

For notational clarity, we will write
(V,\{\mu_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}},\langle-,-\rangle_{V})
as
(V,\mu_{n},\langle-,-\rangle_{V})
.
,
\mathfrak{L}\coloneqq(V,\{\mu_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}},\langle-,-\rangle_{V})
, consists of a graded vector space
V=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}V^{k}
equipped with higher
n
-ary brackets,
\mu_{n}:V\times\cdots\times V\rightarrow V
, and cyclic inner-product (or structure)
\langle-,-\rangle_{V}:V\times V\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, generalising the binary Lie bracket and Cartan–Killing form of Lie algebras. The cyclic structure and higher brackets canonically yield a homotopy Maurer–Cartan action, which is precisely the classical Batalin–Vilkovisky (BV) action of the associated theory. Roughly speaking, the cyclic structure
\langle-,-\rangle_{V}
is an inner-product on the space of (anti)fields that yields the action
2
2

2

Typically integration over the spacetime manifold together with an invariant inner-product on the space of internal symmetry representations carried by the fields.
, the unary bracket
\mu_{1}
encodes the kinetic term of the action, given by
\langle\phi,\mu_{1}(\phi)\rangle_{V}
, while the higher brackets
\mu_{n}
encode the
(n+1)
-point interaction terms, given by
\langle\phi,\mu_{n}(\phi,\ldots,\phi)\rangle_{V}
.

Cast in this language, semi-classical equivalence between physical theories then amounts to quasi-isomorphisms of cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebras. This language is fruitful in that the previous sentence is but the very beginning of a large and detailed dictionary between physics and homotopy algebras outlined in
Table

1
. In particular, every
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathfrak{L}
is equivalent (i.e. quasi-isomorphic) to a unique
3
3

3

up to
L_{\infty}
-isomorphisms

L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathfrak{L}^{\circ}
which has vanishing
\overset{\circ}{\mu}_{1}
. A representative of this equivalence class is called a
minimal model
. When the cyclic structure is preserved by the quasi-isomorphism, the higher brackets of the minimal model encode precisely the non-trivial part of the corresponding connected
S
-matrix. From this perspective, off-shell Lagrangians and scattering amplitudes are unified as quasi-isomorphic
L_{\infty}
-algebras. This makes it clear that quasi-isomorphisms are the correct notion of equivalence; all quasi-isomorphic Lagrangians are quasi-isomorphic to the same minimal model encoding the unique
S
-matrix.

However, there is an important gap in the above dictionary. The cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebra only contains information about the nontrivial part of the
S
-matrix; information about the identity part of the
S
-matrix must be supplied separately. Lacking the identity component, one cannot simply exponentiate the connected diagrams — after all, the minimal model, by definition, has no
\mu_{1}
and, hence, no ‘propagator’ for the identity part of the
S
-matrix. For Minkowski space-time, this is not a serious loss, of course, since it is the literal identity. However, in the case of perturbation theory on nontrivial spaces such as anti-de Sitter space, this is no longer true and the ‘trivial’ Witten diagrams encode nontrivial information such as the CFT two-point function.

This observation is closely related to more technical aspects of the homotopy Maurer–Cartan action in the presence of boundaries. Firstly, the putative cyclic structure, although well-defined in the absence of a boundary, may fail to be cyclic due to boundary contributions that appear when using integration by parts to establish the required cyclic identities. For the same reason, the canonical homotopy Maurer–Cartan action may differ from the physically preferred action, even if the cyclic structure is well-defined. For example, the canonical homotopy-Maurer–Cartan scalar-field-theory kinetic term is

S_{\text{hMC}}=\tfrac{1}{2}\langle\phi,\mu_{1}(\phi)\rangle+\cdots=\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{M}\operatorname{vol}_{M}\phi\Deltaup\phi+\cdots,

(1)

which differs from the physically relevant action functional
\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}\operatorname{vol}_{M}(\partial\phi)^{2}
by a boundary term coming from the total derivative; indeed, this term is precisely that which appears in the computation of the CFT two-point function. Generically, for technical reasons, in the
L_{\infty}
-algebraic approach to perturbation theory
[
2
,
3
,
4
,
5
,
6
,
7
,
8
,
9
,
10
]
, the cyclic structure is defined piecewise for the on-shell,
\mu_{1}(\phi)=0
, and off-shell,
\mu_{1}(\phi)\not=0
, components. So, if we decompose
\phi
it into off-shell and on-shell components
\phi\eqqcolon F+f
respectively, the linearised homotopy Maurer–Cartan action is

S_{\text{hMC}}=\tfrac{1}{2}\langle\phi,\mu_{1}(\phi)\rangle=\tfrac{1}{2}\langle F,\mu_{1}(F)\rangle,

(2)

so that there is no contribution from the on-shell part
f
. However, as we have seen in the scalar field example, the on-shell part can contribute to the physically relevant action functional.

All these observations are uniformly addressed by transitioning from a cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebra to a
relative
cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebra that induces a canonical
relative
homotopy Maurer–Cartan action, which form the central part of this work. A relative cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebra is simply a pair of cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebras,
\mathfrak{L}
and
\mathfrak{L}_{\partial}
, with a cyclic morphism
\piup:\mathfrak{L}\rightarrow\mathfrak{L}_{\partial}
relating them. This is the homotopy relaxation, via Koszul duality, of relative metric Lie algebras, i.e. pairs of homomorphic Lie algebras
\piup:\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}_{\partial}
equipped with inner-products preserved by
\piup
. The key idea is to supplement the original cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathfrak{L}
, the ‘bulk’, with another cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathfrak{L}_{\partial}
, the ‘boundary’. The raison d’être of the boundary
L_{\infty}
-algebra is to simultaneously correct the failure of cyclicity while introducing the physically relevant boundary terms that are not present in the canonical homotopy Maurer–Cartan action. In particular, the bulk-to-boundary morphism
\piup
generates the boundary terms in the relative homotopy Maurer–Cartan action. Every relative
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\piup:\mathfrak{L}\rightarrow\mathfrak{L}_{\partial}
is equivalent to a minimal relative
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\overset{\circ}{\piup}:\mathfrak{L}^{\circ}\rightarrow\mathfrak{L}^{\circ}_{\partial}
. As before, the higher brackets,
\overset{\circ}{\mu}_{k}
, of the minimal model encode the non-trivial connected
S
-matrix, while the ‘trivial’ part is recovered from boundary contributions to the relative minimal model given by
\overset{\circ}{\piup}
. In conclusion, we thus arrive at an abstract structure, relative cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebras, that encodes the physics associated to (asymptotic) boundaries, from
S
-matrices to Witten diagrams, uniformly.

Related works.

The work
[
32
]
is similar in spirit to our discussion of
L_{\infty}
-algebras for theories with boundary in
Section

2.1
and holography in
Section

3.1.2
and, in part, inspired the present contribution. However, it differs substantially in the technical approach. It would be interesting to understand how these (at least superficially) distinct perspectives are related.

In particular, rather than recovering the boundary contributions to the BV-action via the pullback from the boundary BFV theory, as would follow from the standard BV–BFV formalism
[
33
]
, the bulk and boundary are treated within a single
L_{\infty}
-algebra in
[
32
]
, with modified antifields, products and cyclic structure. In particular, the space of antifields is enlarged to include boundary antifields and the differential
\mu_{1}
and cyclic structure are adjusted to reproduce the desired bulk action. It is then shown to be possible to homotopy transfer to a boundary theory, which corresponds to a certain
non-minimal
quasi-isomorphic
L_{\infty}
-algebra. The corresponding homotopy Maurer–Cartan action then computes the boundary action for on-shell field configurations, precisely as one would like for holography. However, the resulting boundary theory is
not
the minimal model of the modified
L_{\infty}
-algebra, since it has non-trivial differential, see
[
32
, (3.38)]
. It is possible, then, that the minimal model itself is actually trivial with no physical fields
4
4

4

For example, for scalar field theory on an oriented compact Riemannian manifold
M
with boundary
\partial M
, then
[
32
, (3.31)]
has as the cochain complex

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)\oplus\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\partial M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Deltaup-m^{2},\partial_{N})}

(3)

where
\Deltaup
is the Beltrami Laplacian, and
\partial_{N}
the normal derivative on
\partial M
. The space of physical fields in the minimal model is given by
\ker(\Deltaup-m^{2},\partial_{N})
. Since the Laplacian on a compact Riemannian manifold with boundary
\partial M
has a non-positive point spectrum for Neumann boundary conditions (
\partial_{N}\phi=0
), see e.g.
[
34
]
, for
m\not=0
the cohomology containing the physical states is trivial.
and thus vanishing boundary action. Correspondingly, it is not possible to encode the identity component of the
S
-matrix, or the two-point function of the boundary CFT in a holographic context, in terms of the minimal model.

By contrast, we prefer to regard invariance under quasi-isomorphisms as a fundamental guiding principle: quasi-isomorphic
L_{\infty}
-algebras should be physically equivalent. The physics, e.g. the
S
-matrix or Witten diagrams, is captured by the relative minimal model, which is unique up to
L_{\infty}
-isomorphisms, with the boundary data encoded in the minimal model morphism
\overset{\circ}{\piup}
.

Our work can be related to the BV–BFV formalism as developed in
[
33
,
35
,
36
]
, but the discussion of the minimal model and scattering amplitudes thereof is new to the best of our knowledge. Note that our discussion of holography focuses on the perturbative sector of Witten diagrams and, as such, differs from (and is complementary to) the holography-related discussion in
[
35
]
, which discusses the nonperturbative aspects of AdS
3
/CFT
2
in particular.

The work
[
37
]
discusses the perturbative aspects of AdS
3
/CFT
2
using the Batalin–Vilkovisky formalism; our discussion is related but complementary in that we connect the BV formulation of holography to
L_{\infty}
-algebras.

The programme by
[
38
,
39
]
to formulate defects and holography using Koszul duality shares many keywords with the current work — Koszul duality, for example, underlies the definition of
L_{\infty}
-algebras — but otherwise differs very much technically; homotopy algebras do not feature heavily in that programme. Nevertheless, it would be interesting to see if the commonalities in concepts could be extended to some sort of concrete connection.

2
Relative
L_{\infty}
-algebras

2.1
A motivating example

Let
M
be an oriented compact Riemannian manifold with metric
g
and boundary
\partial M
. Consider a scalar field
\phi
of mass
m
on
M
governed by the action

S\coloneqq-\int_{M}\operatorname{vol}_{M}\Big\{\tfrac{1}{2}(\partial\phi)^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}m^{2}\phi^{2}+\tfrac{1}{3!}\lambda\phi^{3}\Big\},

(4)

where
\operatorname{vol}_{M}
is the volume form associated with
g
and
\lambda
is the cubic self-interaction coupling constant. The variation of (
4
) with the Dirichlet boundary condition
\deltait\phi|_{\partial M}=0
yields the desired equation of motion

(\Deltaup-m^{2})\phi=\lambda\phi^{2}.

(5)

To encode this theory in terms of an
L_{\infty}
-algebra, as loosely described in
Section

1
, one first integrates by parts in (
4
) to arrive at

S=\int_{M}\operatorname{vol}_{M}\Big\{\tfrac{1}{2}\phi(\Deltaup-m^{2})\phi-\tfrac{1}{3!}\lambda\phi^{3}\Big\}-\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{\partial M}\operatorname{vol}_{\partial M}\phi\partial_{N}\phi.

(6)

Here,
\Deltaup
is the Beltrami Laplacian associated with
g
and
\partial_{N}\colon\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\partial M)
the normal derivative to
\partial M
with respect to
g
.
5
5

5

More precisely, one extends the normal vector field
\partial_{N}
on
\partial M\hookrightarrow M
to some vector field
\tilde{V}_{N}
on
M
in an arbitrary but smooth fashion. Then,
\partial_{N}\phi\coloneqq\tilde{V}_{N}\phi|_{\partial M}
. Note that this does not depend on the choice of the extension
\tilde{V}_{N}
of
\partial_{N}
.
Note that the boundary term
\int_{\partial M}\operatorname{vol}_{\partial M}\phi\partial_{N}\phi
is precisely what is needed to reproduce the equation of motion (
5
) via the variation of (
6
) whilst imposing only the Dirichlet boundary condition on the variation,
\deltait\phi|_{\partial M}=0
. Whilst essentially trivial, this toy example captures the generic situation that an action with higher-than-first-order derivatives of a field requires a boundary correction, the most famous instance of which is the Gibbons–Hawking–York term.

In the present context, the key observation is that the
bulk
term, that is, the first summand in (
6
), can be recast as a homotopy Maurer–Cartan action

S_{\mathrm{hMC}}\coloneqq\sum_{n\geq 2}\frac{1}{n!}\langle\phi,\mu_{n-1}(\phi,\dots,\phi)\rangle

(7)

for the
L_{\infty}
-algebra which has

V\coloneqq\underbrace{\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)}_{\eqqcolon V^{1}}\oplus\underbrace{\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)}_{\eqqcolon V^{2}}

(8a)

as its underlying graded vector space, where the fields
\phi
live in
V^{1}
and the corresponding antifields
\phi^{+}
belong to
V^{2}
. The non-trivial
L_{\infty}
-products
6
6

6

For simplicity we consider only
\phi^{3}
interactions given by
\mu_{2}
, so that the
L_{\infty}
-algebra is merely a graded differential Lie algebra, but arbitrary interactions may be included via higher products
\mu_{n}(\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\ldots,\phi_{n})\coloneqq-\lambda_{n}\phi_{1}\phi_{2}\cdots\phi_{n}
of degree
n-2
.

\mu_{1}(\phi)\coloneqq(\Deltaup-m^{2})\phi\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mu_{2}(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})\coloneqq-\lambda\phi_{1}\phi_{2},

(8b)

of degrees
1
and
0
, respectively, and the non-degenerate bilinear form

\langle\phi,\phi^{\prime+}\rangle_{V}\coloneqq\int_{M}\operatorname{vol}_{M}\phi\phi^{\prime+}

(8c)

of degree
-3
, which pairs fields with antifields. The latter is not cyclic for
\mu_{1}
because of the presence of the boundary
\partial M
, cf.
[
32
]
.

Can we describe the
boundary
term, that is, the second term in (
6
), in a similar language? Naively, we have the boundary-related structures of boundary fields

V_{\partial}^{\text{naive}}\coloneqq\underbrace{\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\partial M)}_{\eqqcolon(V_{\partial}^{\text{naive}})^{1}}

(9a)

and the bulk-to-boundary maps

\begin{array}[]{cccccc}\iota^{*}\,:&V&\rightarrow&V^{\text{naive}}_{\partial},\\
&\begin{pmatrix}\phi\\
\phi^{+}\end{pmatrix}&\mapsto&\phi|_{\partial M},\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[]{cccccc}\partial_{N}\,:&V&\rightarrow&V^{\text{naive}}_{\partial},\\
&\begin{pmatrix}\phi\\
\phi^{+}\end{pmatrix}&\mapsto&\partial_{N}\phi,\end{array}

(9b)

given by the pull-back of the natural inclusion
\iota:V^{\text{naive}}_{\partial}\rightarrow V
and the normal derivative.

There is also the natural non-degenerate bilinear form on
V^{\text{naive}}_{\partial}

\langle\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\rangle_{V^{\text{naive}}_{\partial}}\coloneqq\int_{\partial M}\operatorname{vol}_{\partial M}\alpha\alpha^{\prime}

(9c)

of degree
-2
. With these additional boundary structures it is straightforward to recast the entire action (
6
) as

S=\sum_{n\geq 1}\tfrac{1}{n!}\langle\phi,\mu_{n}(\phi,\ldots,\phi)\rangle_{V}-\tfrac{1}{2}\big\langle\phi|_{\partial M},\partial_{N}\phi\big\rangle_{V^{\text{naive}}_{\partial}}.

(10)

The addition of boundary functions
C^{\infty}(\partial M)
appears in
L_{\infty}
-algebra of
[
32
]
for the same reason. However, in that case it is included in the degree
2
(antifield) component of the original
L_{\infty}
-algebra (and is not doubled, as we shall momentarily describe), while we will place it in a relative boundary
L_{\infty}
-algebra. Of course, since
C^{\infty}(\partial M)
is added as a direct sum in
[
32
]
this is superficially identical (but the degrees are different; here
C^{\infty}(\partial M)
is the space of boundary
fields
).

The presence of
V_{\partial}^{\text{naive}}
and
\langle-,-\rangle_{V^{\text{naive}}_{\partial}}
is reminiscent of the BV–BFV formalism
[
33
,
35
,
36
]
.
The BV–BFV formalism suggests, however, that the boundary should be described by a phase space, not a configuration space — that is, it should be double the size — and that
the two maps
\iota^{*},\partial_{N}\colon V\rightarrow V_{\partial}^{\text{naive}}
should be bundled up into a single map into a phase space.
That is, the equations of motion following from the action (
6
) are of second order so that rather considering the boundary
\partial M
we should be considering the first-order infinitesimal thickening along the normal bundle. Consequently, we take

V_{\partial}\coloneqq\underbrace{\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\partial M)\oplus\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\partial M)}_{\eqqcolon V_{\partial}^{1}}

(11)

instead of (
9
), and combine
\iota^{*}
and
\partial_{N}
into a single linear map of degree
0
,

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\piup\,:&V&\rightarrow&V_{\partial},\\
&\begin{pmatrix}\phi\\
\phi^{+}\end{pmatrix}&\mapsto&\begin{pmatrix}\phi|_{\partial M}\\
-\partial_{N}\phi\end{pmatrix}.\end{array}

(12)

Define a degree
-2
bilinear form,
\langle-,-\rangle_{\partial}:V_{\partial}\times V_{\partial}\rightarrow V_{\partial}
, by

\left\langle\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\alpha^{\prime}\\
\beta^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}\right\rangle_{V_{\partial}}\coloneqq\int_{\partial M}\operatorname{vol}_{\partial M}\,\alpha\beta^{\prime}.

(13)

It then follows that

\tfrac{1}{2}\langle\piup(\phi),\piup(\phi)\rangle_{V_{\partial}}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{\partial M}\operatorname{vol}_{\partial M}\phi\partial_{N}\phi

(14)

reproduces the boundary term in the action (
6
).

Note that the term
\int_{\partial M}\operatorname{vol}_{\partial M}\,\alpha\beta^{\prime}
is also included in the modified cyclic structure of
[
32
]
for precisely the same reason. However, its definition and interpretation differs from (
13
). Specifically, in
[
32
]
it is the space of antifields
V^{2}
of the
L_{\infty}
-algebra (
8
) that is enlarged to include a
single
copy of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\partial M)
, while in the present case we introduce a second boundary
L_{\infty}
-algebra with
V_{\partial}=V_{\partial}^{1}\coloneqq{\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\partial M)\oplus\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\partial M)}
concentrated in the space of
fields
.

We note that the bilinear form (
13
) is degenerate and, in addition, it has no symmetry properties. However, its graded symmetrisation and antisymmetrisation,

\displaystyle\langle x,x^{\prime}\rangle_{V_{\partial}}^{\mathrm{sym}}\coloneqq\tfrac{1}{2}\big(\langle x,x^{\prime}\rangle_{V_{\partial}}+(-1)^{|x||x^{\prime}|}\langle x^{\prime},x\rangle_{V_{\partial}}\big),

(15)

\displaystyle\langle x,x^{\prime}\rangle_{V_{\partial}}^{\mathrm{skew}}\coloneqq\tfrac{1}{2}\big(\langle x,x^{\prime}\rangle_{V_{\partial}}-(-1)^{|x||x^{\prime}|}\langle x^{\prime},x\rangle_{V_{\partial}}\big),

are non-degenerate for all
x\coloneqq(\alpha,\beta)
and
x^{\prime}\coloneqq(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
,
7
7

7

Note that, since
V_{\partial}
is concentrated in degree
1
, we always have
|x|=|x^{\prime}|=1
.
and each component separately plays an important role.

First note, setting
m=0
for notational simplicity,

\begin{split}\langle\phi,\mu_{1}(\phi^{\prime})\rangle_{V}&=\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{M}\operatorname{vol}_{M}\phi\Deltaup\phi^{\prime}\\
&=\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{M}\operatorname{vol}_{M}(\Deltaup\phi)\phi^{\prime}+\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{\partial M}\phi|_{\partial M}\partial_{N}\phi^{\prime}-\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{\partial M}\partial_{N}\phi\phi^{\prime}|_{\partial M}\\
&=\langle\phi^{\prime},\mu_{1}(\phi)\rangle_{V}+\langle\piup(\phi),\piup(\phi^{\prime})\rangle_{V_{\partial}}^{\mathrm{sym}},\end{split}

(16)

so that
\langle-,-\rangle_{V_{\partial}}^{\mathrm{sym}}
corrects for the failure of the cyclicity of the bilinear form (
8c
) for the differential
\mu_{1}
of the bulk
L_{\infty}
-algebra (
8
).

Note that on setting
\phi=\phi^{\prime}
the term
\langle\piup(\phi),\piup(\phi^{\prime})\rangle_{V_{\partial}}^{\mathrm{sym}}
appearing in (
16
) vanishes identically. On the other hand, the graded antisymmetric component yields

\langle\piup(\phi),\piup(\phi)\rangle_{V_{\partial}}^{\mathrm{skew}}=\langle\piup(\phi),\piup(\phi)\rangle_{V_{\partial}},

(17)

so that we recover the boundary term in the action (
6
). If one had merely used only
\langle-,-\rangle_{V_{\partial}}^{\mathrm{skew}}
at the outset, the boundary-term of the action would be recovered, but without any correction for cyclicity.

In summary,
\langle-,-\rangle_{V_{\partial}}^{\mathrm{sym}}
corrects for the failure of the cyclicity while
\langle-,-\rangle_{V_{\partial}}^{\mathrm{skew}}
corrects for the difference between the Dirichlet action (
4
) and the homotopy Maurer–Cartan action (
7
).

It remains to determine the compatibility relation between
\piup
and the bulk
L_{\infty}
-algebra products
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
. It follows that

\piup\circ\mu_{1}=0\quad\mbox{and}\quad\piup\circ\mu_{2}=0

(18)

because of degree reasons since
\piup
is of degree
0
and
V_{\partial}
is concentrated in degree
1
. These identities are consistent with the expected functoriality of
\piup
, i.e. it should be a chain map,
\piup\circ\mu_{1}=\mu^{\partial}_{1}\circ\piup
and a Lie algebra homomorphism
\piup(\mu_{2}(x,x^{\prime}))=\mu^{\partial}_{2}(\piup(x),\piup(x^{\prime}))
for all
x
and
x^{\prime}
.

2.2
Basic definitions

In order to formalise the discussion in
Section

2.1
and to set the stage for our later discussion, we now present some basic definitions, motivating them by reference to the key features exposed in
Section

2.1
.

Cyclic relative
L_{\infty}
-algebras.

We start by recalling the notion of a relative Lie algebra.

Definition 1
.

A
relative Lie algebra

(V,[-,-],V_{\partial},[-,-]_{\partial},\piup)
is a pair of Lie algebras
(V,[-,-])
and
(V_{\partial},[-,-]_{\partial})
together with a morphism
\piup\colon(V,[-,-])\rightarrow(V_{\partial},[-,-]_{\partial})
.
A
morphism of relative Lie algebras

(\phi,\phi_{\partial})\colon(V,V_{\partial})\rightarrow(V^{\prime},V^{\prime}_{\partial})
is a commutative square of Lie algebra homomorphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{\partial}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\prime}_{\partial}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\piup}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\piup^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\prime}}

(19)

Remark 2
.

The class of relative Lie algebras forms a two-coloured operad (one colour for
V
, another colour for
V_{\partial}
). Following
[
40
,
41
]
, this coloured operad admits a minimal model, i.e. the corresponding
\infty
-algebra using Koszul duality, which is the following notion.

Definition 3
.

A
relative
L_{\infty}
-algebra

(V,\mu_{n},V_{\partial},\mu^{\partial}_{n},\piup)
is a pair of
L_{\infty}
-algebras
(V,\mu_{n})
,
(V_{\partial},\mu^{\partial}_{n})
together with a morphism
\piup\colon(V,\mu_{n})\rightarrow(V_{\partial},\mu^{\partial}_{n})
. Furthermore, a
morphism of relative
L_{\infty}
-algebras

(\phi,\phi_{\partial})\colon(V,V_{\partial})\rightarrow(V^{\prime},V^{\prime}_{\partial})
is a commutative square of
L_{\infty}
-algebra morphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{\partial}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\prime}_{\partial}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\piup}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\piup^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\prime}}

(20)

Note that a morphism
\piup
of
L_{\infty}
-algebras consists of a family of
n
-linear maps
\piup_{n}:V\times\cdots\times V\rightarrow V
of degree
1-n
. It is then clear from the definition that the scalar field theory described in
Section

2.1
yields a relative
L_{\infty}
-algebra with non-trivial brackets
\mu_{2},\mu_{1},\mu_{1}^{\partial}
and morphism
\piup
with only
\piup_{1}
non-vanishing. In the context of theories on manifolds with boundary, the general case articulated in
Definition

3
allows for arbitrary bulk and boundary interactions,
\mu_{n}
and
\mu^{\partial}_{n}
.
8
8

8

Boundary interactions will arise when there are derivative bulk interactions.

However, in order to formulate action principles including these interactions, we need the notion of a cyclic structure on a relative
L_{\infty}
-algebra.

Definition 4
.

A
cyclic structure
on a relative
L_{\infty}
-algebra

(V,\mu_{n})\xrightarrow{\piup}(V_{\partial},\mu^{\partial}_{n})

(21)

consists of a non-degenerate graded-symmetric bilinear form

\langle-,-\rangle_{V}\colon V\times V\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

(22a)

of degree
-3
and a bilinear form
9
9

9

We do neither assume that this bilinear form is non-degenerate nor has any symmetry properties.

\langle-,-\rangle_{V_{\partial}}\colon V_{\partial}\times V_{\partial}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

(22b)

of degree
-2
such that for each
n
the multilinear maps

\displaystyle V\times\cdots\times V

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathbb{R},

(22c)

\displaystyle(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})

\displaystyle\mapsto[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]_{V}\coloneqq\langle x_{1},\mu_{n-1}(x_{2},\ldots,x_{n})\rangle_{V}

\displaystyle+\sum_{i+j=n}\langle\piup_{i}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{i}),\piup_{j}(x_{i+1},\ldots,x_{n})\rangle_{V_{\partial}}

and

\displaystyle V_{\partial}\times\cdots\times V_{\partial}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathbb{R},

(22d)

\displaystyle(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})

\displaystyle\mapsto[[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]]_{V_{\partial}}\coloneqq\langle x_{1},\mu^{\partial}_{n-1}(x_{2},\ldots,x_{n})\rangle_{V_{\partial}}

\displaystyle+(-1)^{|x_{1}||\mu^{\partial}_{n-1}(x_{2},\ldots,x_{n})|}\langle\mu^{\partial}_{n-1}(x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}),x_{1}\rangle_{V_{\partial}}

are non-degenerate and also cyclic,

\displaystyle{}_{V}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n-1+(n-1)(|x_{1}|+|x_{n}|)+|x_{n}|\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}|x_{i}|}[x_{n},x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-1}]_{V},

(22e)

\displaystyle[[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]]_{V_{\partial}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n-1+(n-1)(|x_{1}|+|x_{n}|)+|x_{n}|\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}|x_{i}|}[[x_{n},x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-1}]]_{V_{\partial}}.

Note that the cyclicity will directly impose crossing (Bose) symmetry of scattering amplitudes.

Relative homotopy Maurer–Cartan action.

Consider a cyclic relative
L_{\infty}
-algebra

(V,\mu_{n},\langle-,-\rangle_{V})\xrightarrow{\piup}(V_{\partial},\mu^{\partial}_{n},\langle-,-\rangle_{V_{\partial}}).

(23)

Motivated by our discussion in
Section

2.1
, we shall refer to
\mathfrak{L}=(V,\mu_{n},\langle-,-\rangle_{V})
as the
bulk

L_{\infty}
-algebra and to
\mathfrak{L}_{\partial}=(V_{\partial},\mu^{\partial}_{n},\langle-,-\rangle_{V_{\partial}})
as the
boundary

L_{\infty}
-algebra, respectively. To formulate an action principle that encodes all the fields, ghosts, ghosts-for-ghosts, etc. and also their antifields, it is convenient to consider the degree shift by
[1]
of (
23
).
10
10

10

An alternative approach would be to use coordinate functions and the superfield trick
[
42
]
; see
[
14
, §2.1]
for a review.
In particular, we have the identifications
V[1]\cong[1]\otimes V
and
V_{\partial}[1]\cong[1]\otimes V_{\partial}
as graded vector spaces, and upon setting

\displaystyle\tilde{\mu}_{n}([1]\otimes x_{1},\ldots,[1]\otimes x_{n})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\begin{cases}-\mu_{1}(x_{1})&\mbox{for}\quad n=1\\
(-1)^{n+\sum_{i=2}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}|x_{j}|}\mu_{n}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})&\mbox{else}\end{cases},

(24a)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mu}_{n}^{\partial}([1]\otimes x_{1},\ldots,[1]\otimes x_{n})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\begin{cases}-\mu_{1}^{\partial}(x_{1})&\mbox{for}\quad n=1\\
(-1)^{n+\sum_{i=2}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}|x_{j}|}\mu_{n}^{\partial}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})&\mbox{else}\end{cases}

and

\displaystyle\langle[1]\otimes x_{1},[1]\otimes x_{2}\rangle_{V[1]}

\displaystyle\coloneqq(-1)^{|x_{1}|}\langle x_{1},x_{2}\rangle_{V},

(24b)

\displaystyle\langle[1]\otimes x_{1},[1]\otimes x_{2}\rangle_{V_{\partial}[1]}

\displaystyle\coloneqq(-1)^{|x_{1}|}\langle x_{1},x_{2}\rangle_{V_{\partial}}

as well as

\tilde{\piup}([1]\otimes x)\coloneqq[1]\otimes\piup(x),

(24c)

we obtain the degree-shifted cyclic relative
L_{\infty}
-algebra

(V[1],\tilde{\mu}_{n},\langle-,-\rangle_{V[1]})\xrightarrow{\tilde{\piup}}(V_{\partial}[1],\tilde{\mu}^{\partial}_{n},\langle-,-\rangle_{V_{\partial}[1]}).

(24d)

Definition 5
.

Consider the degree-shifted cyclic relative
L_{\infty}
-algebra (
24d
). The associated
relative homotopy Maurer–Cartan action
is the expression

\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{rhMC}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{n\geq 2}\frac{1}{n!}[x,\ldots,x]_{V[1]}

(25)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\geq 2}\frac{1}{n!}\Big(\langle x,\tilde{\mu}_{n-1}(x,\ldots,x)\rangle_{V[1]}+\sum_{i+j=n}\langle\tilde{\piup}_{i}(x,\ldots,x),\tilde{\piup}_{j}(x,\ldots,x)\rangle_{V_{\partial}[1]}\Big)

for all
x\in V[1]
.

Evidently, the first term in (
25
) is the standard homotopy Maurer–Cartan action (
7
), and this will be the only term if the manifold on which the fields live has no boundary. In this case,
\langle-,-\rangle_{V}
will be cyclic. Generally, if we have a boundary, the second term will be present. However, if there are no derivative interactions, only
\piup_{1}
will be non-trivial.

Remark 6
.

Consider a pair of cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebras
(V,\mu_{n},\langle-,-\rangle_{V})
and
(V^{\prime},\mu^{\prime}_{n},\langle-,-\rangle_{V^{\prime}})
, that is, both
\langle-,-\rangle_{V}
and
\langle-,-\rangle_{V^{\prime}}
are assumed to be cyclic. Recall that a morphism

(V,\mu_{n},\langle-,-\rangle_{V})\xrightarrow{\piup}(V^{\prime},\mu^{\prime}_{n},\langle-,-\rangle_{V^{\prime}})

(26)

is called
cyclic

[
43
]
(see also
[
14
,
15
]
) provided that

\langle x_{1},x_{2}\rangle_{V}=\langle\piup_{1}(x_{1}),\piup_{1}(x_{2})\rangle_{V^{\prime}}

(27a)

and

\sum_{i+j=n}\langle\piup_{i}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{i}),\piup_{j}(x_{i+1},\ldots,x_{i+j})\rangle_{V^{\prime}}=0

(27b)

for all
n\geq 3
. Consequently, upon requiring the morphism
\piup
entering the
Definitions

3
and
4
to be cyclic in this sense, the second term in the relative homotopy Maurer–Cartan action (
25
) drops out, and we are in the standard situation where we do not have any boundary contributions.
11
11

11

Note that in this case the condition (
27a
) means that
\langle\tilde{\piup}_{1}(\phi),\tilde{\piup}_{1}(\phi)\rangle_{V_{\partial}[1]}=0
for degree reasons.

2.3
Relation to BV–BFV formalism

A natural formalism for describing systems with boundaries is given by the Batalin–Vilkovisky–Batalin–Fradkin–Vilkovisky (BV–BFV) formalism
[
33
,
35
,
36
]
. It uses the language of graded manifolds, for which see
[
14
,
15
,
44
,
45
]
. The central notion of the BV–BFV formalism is that of a BV–BFV pair.

Definition 7
.

A
BV–BFV pair

(X,Q,\omega)\xrightarrow{\Pi}(X_{\partial},Q_{\partial},\omega_{\partial})
consists of a pair of differential graded manifolds
(X,Q)
and
(X_{\partial},Q_{\partial})
together with a morphism
\Pi\colon(X,Q)\rightarrow(X_{\partial},Q_{\partial})
and closed two-forms
\omega\in\Omegaup^{2}(X)
and
\omega_{\partial}\in\Omegaup^{2}(X_{\partial})
such that

\mathcal{L}_{Q}\omega+\Pi^{*}\omega_{\partial}=0\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathcal{L}_{Q_{\partial}}\omega_{\partial}=0.

(28)

Here,
\mathcal{L}
denotes the Lie derivative.

Our notion of relative
L_{\infty}
-algebras relates to the BV–BFV formalism as follows. Firstly, recall that an
L_{\infty}
-algebra
(V,\mu_{n})
can equivalently be described as the differential graded manifold
(V[1],Q)
where the homological vector field
Q
encodes the products
\mu_{n}
. In addition, a cyclic structure
\langle-,-\rangle_{V}
of degree
k
on
V
is equivalent to a constant (and hence, closed) non-degenerate two-form
\omega\in\Omegaup^{2}(V[1])
of degree
k+2
such that
\mathcal{L}_{Q}\omega=0
. We can extend this as follows.

Proposition 8
.

Given a cyclic relative
L_{\infty}
-algebra

(V,\mu_{n},\langle-,-\rangle_{V})\xrightarrow{\piup}(V_{\partial},\mu^{\partial}_{n},\langle-,-\rangle_{V_{\partial}}),

(29)

there exists a BV–BFV pair

(V[1],Q,\omega)\xrightarrow{\Piit}(V_{\partial}[1],Q_{\partial},\omega_{\partial}),

(30)

where
(V[1],Q)
and
(V_{\partial}[1],Q_{\partial})
are differential graded manifolds encoding the
L_{\infty}
-algebras
(V,\mu_{n})
and
(V_{\partial},\mu^{\partial}_{n})
, respectively, and a morphism
\Piit\colon(V[1],Q)\rightarrow(V_{\partial}[1],Q_{\partial})
encoding
\piup
and where
\omega\in\Omegaup^{2}(V[1])
and
\omega_{\partial}\in\Omegaup^{2}(V_{\partial}[1])
are the constant two-forms of degrees
-1
and
0
, respectively and encoding
\langle-,-\rangle_{V}
and (the graded-symmetric part of)
\langle-,-\rangle_{V_{\partial}}
, respectively.

Proof.

The conditions (
28
) are simply the conditions for a cyclic structure in
Definition

4
.
∎

2.4
Minimal model and generalised scattering amplitudes

Since we seek to describe scattering amplitudes, we need to consider the minimal model of a relative
L_{\infty}
-algebra. Since relative
L_{\infty}
-algebras are special cases of coloured
\infty
-algebras defined by Koszul duality, they enjoy homotopy transfer theorems; in particular, their minimal models exist. Preserving the cyclic structure requires more work, cf.
[
46
,
47
]
.

Construction of the minimal model.

We can construct the minimal model explicitly as follows. Given a cyclic relative
L_{\infty}
-algebra
(V,\mu_{n})\xrightarrow{\piup}(V_{\partial},\mu_{n}^{\partial})
and deformation retracts

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V,\mu_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(H^{\bullet}(V),0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{\partial},\mu_{1}^{\partial})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(H^{\bullet}(V_{\partial}),0)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\piup}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}^{\partial}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{\partial}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{1}^{\partial}}

(31)

by means of the homological perturbation lemma (see e.g.
[
48
,
49
,
50
]
) we obtain the
L_{\infty}
-morphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V,\mu_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(H^{\bullet}(V),\{\overset{\circ}{\mu}{}_{n>1}\})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{\partial},\mu^{\partial}_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(H^{\bullet}(V_{\partial}),\{\overset{\circ}{\mu}{}_{n>1}^{\partial}\})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\piup}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overset{\circ}{\piup}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\partial}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\partial}}

(32a)

with
i
,
p
,
i^{\partial}
, and
p^{\partial}
being
L_{\infty}
-quasi-isomorphisms and

\overset{\circ}{\piup}\coloneqq p_{\partial}\circ\piup\circ i.

(32b)

We call

(H^{\bullet}(V),\overset{\circ}{\mu}{}_{n>1})\xrightarrow{\overset{\circ}{\piup}}(H^{\bullet}(V_{\partial}),\overset{\circ}{\mu}{}_{n>1}^{\partial})

(33)

the
minimal model
of
(V,\mu_{n})\xrightarrow{\piup}(V_{\partial},\mu_{n}^{\partial})
.

Explicitly, we have the following recursion relations expressing the maps
\overset{\circ}{\mu}_{i}
,
\overset{\circ}{\mu}{}^{\partial}_{i}
,
\overset{\circ}{\piup}_{i}
of the minimal model in terms of the original maps
\mu_{i}
,
\mu^{\partial}_{i}
,
\piup_{i}
.

The first two bulk minimal model
L_{\infty}
-brackets are given by

\overset{\circ}{\mu}_{2}

(34d)

Again, there is the obvious generalisation to higher brackets and Feynman diagrammatic interpretation.

More novel is that the bulk and boundary minimal models are connected by the minimal model bulk-to-boundary morphisms

(36)

Of the diagrams that comprise
S_{\text{conn}}
, the ‘trivial’ diagram ‘
|
’ is special. The minimal model of an
L_{\infty}
-algebra contains the data for all connected diagrams
except
for the trivial diagram, but the trivial diagram is crucial in reproducing the full
S
-matrix. And, in fact, the ‘trivial’ diagram is not so trivial in general in curved geometries such as anti-de Sitter space.

Definition 9
.

Consider the minimal model (
33
) of a relative cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebra as well its degree shift constructed by means of (
24
). At the tree level, the associated
generalised connected
n
-point scattering amplitude
is given by the expression

\displaystyle{}_{H^{\bullet}(V)[1]}

\displaystyle=\langle\phi_{1},\tilde{\overset{\circ}{\mu}}_{n-1}(\phi_{2},\ldots,\phi_{n})\rangle_{H^{\bullet}(V)}

(37)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i+j=n}\langle\tilde{\overset{\circ}{\piup}}_{i}(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{i}),\tilde{\overset{\circ}{\piup}}_{j}(\phi_{i+1},\ldots,\phi_{n})\rangle_{H^{\bullet}(V_{\partial})}

for
\phi_{1,\ldots,n}\in H^{\bullet}(V)[1]
.
12
12

12

Put differently, the generalised scattering amplitudes follow from the polarisation of the relative homotopy Maurer–Cartan action (
25
) for the degree-shifted minimal model of a relative cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebra.

Note that, whilst
\langle-,-\rangle_{H^{\bullet}(V)}
need not be cyclic with respect to
\overset{\circ}{\mu}_{i}
, any failure of cyclicity is compensated by the antisymmetric part of
\langle-,-\rangle_{H^{\bullet}(V_{\partial})}
according to
Definition

4
, such that the
n
-point connected scattering amplitudes are guaranteed to respect crossing (Bose) symmetry. Note also that, for the two-point scattering amplitude, whilst the first term in (
37
) vanishes since
\overset{\circ}{\mu}_{1}=0
by construction, the second term will, in general, not be zero, so that we recover the trivial piece of the
S
-matrix.

The full (rather than connected) tree-level
S
-matrix can be easily defined in terms of the connected tree-level
S
-matrix. For the full quantum
S
-matrix, there is an evident generalisation to the loop case following
[
5
,
8
]
, where one should consider a loop relative
L_{\infty}
-algebra.

Boundary contributions to higher-point scattering amplitudes.

In the above, suppose that
\piup
is strict, that is,
\piup_{n>1}=0
and that

\piup_{1}\circ h=0

(38)

for
h
the contracting homotopy. According to explicit formulas for homotopy transfer, the higher-order components
i_{n>1}
of the
L_{\infty}
-quasi-isomorphism
i
in (
32
) satisfy

\operatorname{im}(i_{n})\subseteq\operatorname{im}(h).

(39)

Then it follows that the boundary terms in (
37
) are trivial except for two-point scattering amplitudes. Therefore, in this case, we only have boundary corrections to the two-point scattering amplitudes but not to higher-point ones, consistent with the fact that the usual
L_{\infty}
-algebra formalism (ignoring boundary contributions) yield the correct connected tree-level scattering amplitudes for
n>2
points.

3
Examples

Let us now discuss applications of the above setup. We start off considering the simplest examples of scalar field theory, before moving on to Chern–Simons theory and Yang–Mills theory.

3.1
Scalar field theory on a manifold-with-boundary

Having setting out the definitions in
Section

2.2
, we can now return to the motivating example from
Section

2.1
, first concisely summarising the general setup for compact Riemannian manifold with boundary and then presenting the corresponding relative minimal model.

We then consider the more subtle cases of Euclidean flat and anti-de Sitter space, where the boundaries are asymptotic. In the latter case, we shall reproduce the well-known results from the AdS/CFT literature. See also
[
32
]
for analogous results, obtained in a conceptually similar manner (although, since our underlying framework is different, the technical details remain distinct).

3.1.1
Compact Riemannian manifolds-with-boundary

Consider a scalar field on scalar field
\phi
of mass
m
on an oriented compact Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
with boundary
\partial M
and cubic interactions.

Relative
L_{\infty}
-algebra.

The associated cochain complex of the relative
L_{\infty}
-algebra is
13
13

13

This complex differs from the analogous structures in
[
32
]
(see eqq. (3.31) and (3.38) therein) in two regards. Firstly, the ‘bulk’ and ‘boundary’ (anti)fields are contained in single relative
L_{\infty}
-algebra, as opposed to belonging to quasi-isomorphic, but distinct,
L_{\infty}
-algebras related by homotopy retract data. Indeed, we have a homotopy transfer from the full relative bulk
+
boundary relative
L_{\infty}
-algebra to a minimal bulk
+
boundary relative
L_{\infty}
-algebra. Secondly, the boundary fields are doubled and the space of boundary antifields is trivial.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}(\partial M)\oplus C^{\infty}(\partial M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\piup_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

(40a)

where the first row represents
V
and the second row
V_{\partial}
in the notation of
Section

2.2
. The non-trivial brackets and components of
\piup
are

\begin{gathered}\mu_{1}(\phi)\coloneqq(\Deltaup-m^{2})\phi,\quad\mu_{2}(\phi,\phi^{\prime})\coloneqq-\lambda\phi\phi^{\prime},\\
\piup_{1}(\phi)\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}\phi|_{\partial M}\\
-\partial_{N}\phi\end{pmatrix},\end{gathered}

(40b)

where
\lambda
is the coupling constant. As in
Section

2.1
,
\partial_{N}
is (the differential operator associated with) the vector field normal to
\partial M
, and
\phi|_{\partial M}
is the restriction of
\phi
to
\partial M
. Note that the doubled function space in
V_{\partial}
can be seen as the space of functions on a first-order neighbourhood of
\partial M
. Furthermore, we set

\langle\phi,\phi^{\prime+}\rangle_{V}\coloneqq\int_{M}\operatorname{vol}_{M}\phi\phi^{\prime+}\eqqcolon\langle\phi^{\prime+},\phi\rangle_{V}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\left\langle\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\alpha^{\prime}\\
\beta^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}\right\rangle_{V_{\partial}}\coloneqq\int_{\partial M}\operatorname{vol}_{\partial M}\alpha\beta^{\prime}.

(40c)

The relative homotopy Maurer–Cartan action (
25
) for this relative
L_{\infty}
-algebra is

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=\int_{M}\operatorname{vol}_{M}\Big\{\tfrac{1}{2}\phi(\Deltaup-m^{2})\phi-\tfrac{1}{3!}\lambda\phi^{3}\Big\}-\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{\partial M}\operatorname{vol}_{\partial M}\phi\partial_{N}\phi

(41)

\displaystyle=-\int_{M}\operatorname{vol}_{M}\Big\{\tfrac{1}{2}(\partial\phi)^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}m^{2}\phi^{2}+\tfrac{1}{3!}\lambda\phi^{3}\Big\}.

Relative minimal model.

The cochain complex of the minimal model of the bulk theory is

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker(\Deltaup-m^{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{coker}(\Deltaup-m^{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overset{\circ}{\mu}_{1}}=0}

(42)

The homotopy
h
is given by a choice of Green’s function
14
14

14

If one imposes Dirichlet boundary conditions the Green’s function is unique.

G_{m}
for
\Deltaup-m^{2}
(understood to carry
L_{\infty}
-degree
-1
). Then, the trivial embeddings of
\ker(\Deltaup-m^{2})
into
C^{\infty}(M)
and the obvious projections
\textrm{id}-\mu_{1}\circ G_{m}:C^{\infty}(M)\rightarrow\ker(\Deltaup-m^{2})
and
\textrm{id}-G_{m}\circ\mu_{1}:C^{\infty}(M)\rightarrow\operatorname{coker}(\Deltaup-m^{2})
provide the homotopy retract data. The higher products
\overset{\circ}{\mu}_{n>1}
then follow from the homological perturbation lemma, using the formulae of
Section

2.4
. See also
[
3
]
for more details.

The boundary minimal model is trivially given by the boundary
L_{\infty}
-algebra itself, so the cochain complex for the relative minimal model is given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker(\Deltaup-m^{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{coker}(\Deltaup-m^{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}(\partial M)\oplus C^{\infty}(\partial M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overset{\circ}{\mu}_{1}}=0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overset{\circ}{\piup}}_{1}=\piup_{1}\big|_{\ker(\Deltaup-m^{2})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overset{\circ}{\piup}_{1}=0}

(43)

where the first row represents
H^{\bullet}(V)
and the second row
H^{\bullet}(V_{\partial})
, and there are no boundary higher products
\overset{\circ}{\mu}{}^{\partial}_{n}
(since there are no derivative interactions in the bulk). The minimal model then encodes Feynman-diagram-like perturbation theory for solutions to the classical equations of motion.

3.1.2
Flat and anti-de Sitter spaces

In this section, we discuss field theories on manifolds with a boundary ‘at infinity’, namely on anti-de Sitter space and flat space, to reproduce conformal-field-theory (CFT) correlators (computed via Witten diagrams) and
S
-matrices (computed via Feynman diagrams), respectively. Our discussion for the flat-space
S
-matrix will be inspired by the AdS/CFT correspondence; for a related approach see
[
51
]
. In both cases, it is more convenient to motivate the construction in Euclidean (rather than Lorentzian) signature, although one can always Wick-rotate to Lorentzian structure afterwards. The idea that the trivial part of the
S
-matrix arises from boundary terms also appears in
[
52
]
.

Euclidean anti-de Sitter space.

Consider the Poincaré patch of Euclidean anti-de Sitter space (a.k.a. hyperbolic space)
{\rm AdS}_{d+1}
. The underlying manifold is
{\rm AdS}_{d+1}\coloneqq\{(z,\vec{y})|z\in\mathbb{R}_{>0},\vec{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
, and the Riemannian metric
g
is given by

g_{\mathrm{AdS}}\coloneqq\frac{1}{z^{2}}\left(\mathrm{d}z\otimes\mathrm{d}z+\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathrm{d}y^{i}\otimes\mathrm{d}y^{i}\right).

(44)

The conformal boundary
S^{d}\cong\mathbb{R}^{d}\cup\infty
lies at
z=0
and
z=\infty
.

Euclidean flat space.

We treat
(d+1)
-dimensional Euclidean flat space similarly to the hyperbolic space: the underlying manifold is
\{(z,\vec{y})|z\in\mathbb{R}_{>0},\vec{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
, and the Riemannian metric
g
is given by

g_{\mathrm{E}}\coloneqq\frac{1}{z^{2}}\mathrm{d}z\otimes\mathrm{d}z+\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathrm{d}y^{i}\otimes\mathrm{d}y^{i}.

(45)

Using the coordinate transformation
z\eqqcolon\mathrm{e}^{t}
, then
(t,\vec{y})
are the usual Cartesian coordinates for
\mathbb{R}^{1+d}
. Since this is in Euclidean signature, there is only one component of the conformal boundary (similar to hyperbolic space), which lies at
z=0
, corresponding to the far past
t\to-\infty
; there is no corresponding boundary component for the far future
t\to+\infty
. Effectively, we are using crossing symmetry to replace outgoing legs of positive energy with incoming legs of negative energy so that all external legs come in from the far past.

To treat the hyperbolic and flat cases uniformly, we write

g\coloneqq\frac{1}{z^{2}}\mathrm{d}z\otimes\mathrm{d}z+\frac{1}{z^{2n}}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathrm{d}y^{i}\otimes\mathrm{d}y^{i}

(46)

with
n=0
for flat space and
n=1
for hyperbolic space, and set
z\eqqcolon\mathrm{e}^{t}
.

Bulk function space.

Let
C
be the space of smooth functions
\phi\colon\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that there exists a function
\phi_{\text{interior}}\colon\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
that decays superpolynomially as
z\to 0
or, equivalently, superexponentially as
t\to-\infty
(i.e.
\lim_{z\to 0}z^{-\alpha}\phi_{\mathrm{interior}}(z,\vec{y})=0
or
\lim_{t\to-\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha t}\phi_{\mathrm{interior}}(\mathrm{e}^{t},\vec{y})=0
at every
\vec{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
) and the difference
\phi_{\mathrm{pw}}\coloneqq\phi-\phi_{\text{interior}}
is a countable
15
15

15

We allow countable, rather than finite, sums so as to include solutions to the Helmholtz equation on hyperbolic space.
sum of functions that depend homogeneously on
z
as

\phi(z,\vec{y})=\phi_{\text{interior}}(z,\vec{y})+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}z^{\alpha_{i}}\phi_{\alpha_{i}}(\vec{y}),

(47)

where
\phi_{\alpha_{i}}\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
are each a compactly supported smooth function and
\alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
. Note that
C
is defined so that it includes, in addition to functions that decay quickly at infinity, on-shell plane waves as well as their products and derivatives; it is not possible to restrict to only on-shell waves since they are not closed under products.

On
C
, wherever convergent, we set

\langle\phi,\phi^{\prime}\rangle_{C}\coloneqq\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}z\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathrm{d}^{d}\vec{y}\,z^{-nd-1}\,\phi(z,\vec{y})\phi^{\prime}(z,\vec{y}),

(48)

using the volume density
\sqrt{\det g}=z^{-nd-1}
.

Boundary function space.

For each
\deltait\in\mathbb{R}
, let
C_{\partial}^{\deltait}
be (a copy of) the function space

C_{\partial}^{\deltait}\coloneqq\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

(49)

of smooth functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
This should be thought of as the space of the ‘values at
z=0
of ‘plane waves’ with ‘dispersion relation’ characterised by
\deltait
.
Let
C_{\partial}
be the space of formal sums

C_{\partial}=\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}(\deltait_{i},\phi_{i})\middle|\phi_{i}\in C_{\partial}^{\deltait_{i}}\right\}

(50)

such that the function

(z,\vec{y})\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}z^{\deltait_{i}}\phi_{i}(\vec{y})

(51)

converges pointwise.

Thus, for the flat space case,
C_{\partial}
is the space of linear combinations of on-shell plane waves. The space
C_{\partial}
is chosen such that it includes the asymptotic values (i.e. ‘values at
z=0
’) for solutions to the Helmholtz equation (for both the flat and hyperbolic cases) for different values of the squared mass
m^{2}
. For the Euclidean case, solutions to the Helmholtz equation are

z^{\pm\sqrt{m^{2}+\vec{p}^{2}}}\exp(\mathrm{i}\vec{p}\cdot\vec{y}),

(52)

which we regard as an element of
C^{\sqrt{m^{2}+\vec{p}^{2}}}_{\partial}
,
while for the hyperbolic case, solutions to the Helmholtz equation are of the form

\phi(z,\vec{y})=z^{\deltait_{-}}\phi_{\deltait_{-}}(\vec{y})+\dotsb+z^{\deltait_{+}}\phi_{\deltait_{+}}(\vec{y})+\dotsb,

(53a)

where

\deltait_{\pm}\coloneqq\tfrac{d}{2}\pm\sqrt{\tfrac{d^{2}}{4}+m^{2}}

(53b)

and
\deltait_{+}
is the conformal dimension of the corresponding CFT local operator,
16
16

16

To avoid confusion with the Beltrami Laplacian, we denote the conformal dimension by
\deltait_{+}
.
and
\phi_{\deltait_{+}}
is related to
\phi_{\deltait_{-}}
as

\phi_{\deltait_{+}}(\vec{y})\propto\int\mathrm{d}^{d}\vec{y}^{\prime}\,\frac{\phi_{\deltait_{-}}(\vec{y}^{\prime})}{|\vec{y}-\vec{y}^{\prime}|^{2\deltait_{+}}}.

(54)

We regard this as the formal sum

(\deltait_{-},\phi_{\deltait_{-}})+\cdots+(\deltait_{+},\phi_{\deltait_{+}})+\cdots\in C_{\partial}.

(55)

Furthermore, on
C_{\partial}
, wherever convergent, we set

\langle f,g\rangle_{C_{\partial}}\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathrm{d}^{d}\vec{y}\,\sum_{\deltait\in\mathbb{R}}f_{\deltait}(\vec{y})g_{1+nd-\deltait}(\vec{y}).

(56)

We also have the map

\displaystyle(-)_{\mathrm{pw}}\colon C

\displaystyle\rightarrow C_{\partial},

(57)

\displaystyle\left(\phi_{\mathrm{interior}}+\sum_{i}z^{\deltait_{i}}\phi_{i}\right)

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{i}(\deltait_{i},\phi_{i}),

where
\phi_{\mathrm{interior}}
was defined in (
47
), which picks out the asymptotic components of
\phi\in C
.

Relative
L_{\infty}
-algebra.

Inspired by our discussion from
Section

3.1
, the relative
L_{\infty}
-algebra for a scalar field
\phi
of mass
m
with cubic interaction is

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\partial}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\piup_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

(58a)

with

\begin{gathered}\mu_{1}(\phi)\coloneqq(\Deltaup-m^{2})\phi,\quad\mu_{2}(\phi,\phi^{\prime})\coloneqq-\lambda\phi\phi^{\prime},\\
\piup_{1}(\phi)\coloneqq\phi_{\text{pw}},\end{gathered}

(58b)

where the Beltrami Laplacian is

\Deltaup\phi\coloneqq-z^{1+nd}\left(\partial_{z}(z^{1-nd}\partial_{z}\phi)+\partial_{\vec{y}}(z^{-1-nd}z^{2n}\partial_{\vec{y}}\phi)\right)

(58c)

and where
c
is a constant; it will eventually be fixed by requiring that the coefficient for the quadratic term in (
69
) or (
71
) below is correctly normalised (when compared to the trivial part of the
S
-matrix or the CFT two-point function). Furthermore,

\langle\phi,\phi^{\prime+}\rangle_{V}\coloneqq\langle\phi,\phi^{\prime+}\rangle_{C}\eqqcolon\langle\phi^{\prime+},\phi\rangle_{V}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\left\langle\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\right\rangle_{V_{\partial}}\coloneqq\langle\alpha,\beta^{\prime}\rangle_{C_{\partial}},

(58d)

where
\langle-,-\rangle_{C}
and
\langle-,-\rangle_{C_{\partial}}
were given in (
48
) and (
56
), respectively.

Using these ingredients, the relative homotopy Maurer–Cartan action (
25
) becomes

S=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{z^{1+nd}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathrm{d}^{d}\vec{y}\Big\{\tfrac{1}{2}\phi(\Deltaup-m^{2})\phi-\tfrac{1}{3!}\lambda\phi^{3}\Big\}+c\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathrm{d}^{d}\vec{y}\,\Big[(z^{-1-nd}\phi(z,\vec{y})(\partial_{z}\phi)(z,\vec{y})\Big]_{0},

(59)

where
[\cdots]_{0}
extracts the component that is of order
\mathcal{O}(z^{0})
. This can be recognised as a regularised version of the standard action

S_{\text{naive}}=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{z^{1+nd}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathrm{d}^{d}\vec{y}\Big\{\tfrac{1}{2}(\partial\phi)^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}m^{2}\phi^{2}+\tfrac{1}{3!}\lambda\phi^{3}\Big\}.

(60)

Deformation retract.

We have the deformation retract
(i,p,h)
whose components are

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\oplus\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\oplus\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\partial}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\partial}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Deltaup-m^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)_{\mathrm{pw}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f_{+},f_{-})\mapsto(i(f_{+},f_{-}))_{\mathrm{pw}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Deltaup-m^{2})^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}

(61)

Here,
i
is given (for both fields and antifields) as

i\,:\,(f_{+},f_{-})\mapsto\int\mathrm{d}^{d}\vec{y}\,(f_{+}(\vec{y})K_{+}(-,-;\vec{y})+f_{-}(\vec{y})K_{-}(-,-;\vec{y}))

(62)

in terms of the bulk-to-boundary propagator

K_{\pm}(\vec{y},z;\vec{y}^{\prime})\coloneqq\begin{cases}\int\mathrm{d}^{d}\vec{p}\,z^{\deltait_{\pm}(\vec{p})}\exp(\mathrm{i}(\vec{y}-\vec{y}^{\prime})\cdot\vec{p})&n=0\\
\frac{\Gammaup(\deltait_{\pm})}{\piup^{d/2}\Gammaup(\deltait_{\mp})}\left(\frac{z}{z^{2}+(\vec{y}-\vec{y}^{\prime})^{2}}\right)^{\deltait_{\pm}}&n=1,\end{cases}

(63)

where

\deltait_{\pm}(\vec{p})\coloneqq\begin{cases}\pm\sqrt{m^{2}+\vec{p}^{2}}&\text{for}\quad n=0\\
\frac{d}{2}\pm\sqrt{\frac{d^{2}}{4}+m^{2}}&\text{for}\quad n=1\end{cases}

(64)

is either the on-shell energy (and its negative) for flat space or the conformal dimension (and its conjugate) of the corresponding CFT operator for hyperbolic space, and where

m^{2}\geq\begin{cases}0&\text{for}\quad n=0\\
-\frac{d^{2}}{4}&\text{for}\quad n=1\end{cases}

(65)

is the squared mass of the particle, which obeys the Breitenlohner–Freedman bound
[
53
,
54
]
in the case of
n=1
and is non-negative for
n=0
.
For the flat space case, the expression is perhaps clearer in momentum space:

\hat{K}_{\pm}(\vec{y},z;\vec{p})=z^{\deltait_{\pm}(\vec{p})}\exp(\mathrm{i}\vec{y}\cdot\vec{p})=\exp\left(\deltait_{\pm}(\vec{p})t+\mathrm{i}\vec{y}\cdot\vec{p}\right),

(66)

which is the Wick-rotated version of an on-shell plane wave.

The projection map
p
is given in terms of a suitable left inverse of
i
.

Minimal model for flat space.

Let us Fourier-transform
\vec{y}
into
\vec{p}
. Then, solutions to the equations of motion are linear combinations of plane waves of the form

\hat{\phi}_{\pm}(z,\vec{p})=z^{\pm E_{\vec{p}}}\hat{\phi}^{\pm}_{E_{\vec{p}}}(\vec{p})

(67a)

where

E_{\vec{p}}\coloneqq\sqrt{m^{2}+\vec{p}^{2}}

(67b)

is the mass-shell condition.

The cochain complex underlying the minimal model for (
58
) is then

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\oplus\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\oplus\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\partial}\oplus C_{\partial}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overset{\circ}{\mu}}_{1}=0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f_{+},f_{-})\mapsto(i(f_{+},f_{-}))_{\mathrm{pw}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

(68)

where all the
\overset{\circ}{\mu}_{n>1}
are present and are given by the homological perturbation lemma as discussed in
Section

2.4
. Furthermore, the relative homotopy Maurer–Cartan action (
25
) for the minimal model is

S=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathrm{d}^{d}\vec{p}\,E_{\vec{p}}\,\hat{\phi}^{+}_{E_{\vec{p}}}(\vec{p})\hat{\phi}^{-}_{E_{\vec{p}}}(-\vec{p})-\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{z}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathrm{d}^{d}\vec{y}\frac{1}{3!}\lambda\phi^{3}(z,\vec{y})+\cdots,

(69)

where
\phi(z,\vec{y})
is a linear combination of the Fourier-transform of (
67
) and the ellipsis denotes higher-order scattering amplitudes via the higher-order products
\overset{\circ}{\mu}_{n>1}
. In particular, the two-point scattering amplitude is seen to reproduce the (Euclidean) Klein–Gordon metric (see e.g.
[
55
, (4.9)]
), correctly pairing positive-energy (incoming) and negative-energy (outgoing) states.

and

Minimal model for anti-de Sitter space.

Let us take the minimal model for anti-de Sitter space, that is, for
n=1
. In this case, solutions to the Helmholtz equation follow the ansatz (
53a
).

As before, the cochain complex underlying the minimal model for (
58
) is

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\oplus\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\oplus\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\partial}\oplus C_{\partial}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overset{\circ}{\mu}}_{1}=0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f_{+},f_{-})\mapsto(i(f_{+},f_{-}))_{\mathrm{pw}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

(70)

with
\overset{\circ}{\mu}_{n>1}
given by the homological perturbation lemma as discussed in
Section

2.4
. Furthermore, the relative homotopy Maurer–Cartan action (
25
) for the minimal model is

S=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\mathrm{d}\vec{y}\,\mathrm{d}\vec{y}^{\prime}\,\frac{\phi_{\deltait_{+}}(\vec{y})\phi_{\deltait_{+}}(\vec{y}^{\prime})}{|\vec{y}-\vec{y}^{\prime}|^{2\deltait_{+}}}-\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{z^{1+d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathrm{d}^{d}\vec{y}\frac{1}{3!}\lambda\phi^{3}(z,\vec{y})+\cdots

(71)

where the ellipsis encodes the higher-order
\overset{\circ}{\mu}_{n>1}
generated by homotopy transfer, corresponding to evaluating the sum over connected Witten diagrams; these encode the connected
(n+1)
-point correlation functions of the boundary CFT. Then, it is clear that the corresponding scattering amplitudes reproduce those of Witten diagrams:

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

(72b)

and so on, where in the homological perturbation lemma we have the correspondence in
Table

2
.

The CFT two-point correlator is
not
given by the usual homotopy transfer but is instead given by
\langle\piup_{1}(-),\piup_{1}(-)\rangle
, that is, from the boundary term of the relative Maurer–Cartan action; this is essentially the classic derivation
[
56
, §2.4,§2.5]
that involves integration by parts of the bulk AdS action to reduce it to a boundary term.
17
17

17

In
[
56
]
this computation is done with a bulk-to-boundary propagator, which provides the required regularisation; the choice of the relative
L_{\infty}
-algebra here encodes an equivalent choice of regulator.
This seeming inhomogeneity is, in fact, natural: the two-point correlator is
not
part of the connected correlator, since the full correlator is obtained by ‘exponentiating’ the connected correlator, whose ‘identity’ component consists purely of Wick contractions involving the two-point correlator (and other Wick contractions).

In this sense, the leftmost diagram in
[
57
, Figure 1]
,

\hbox to58.11pt{\vbox to58.11pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 29.05275pt\lower-29.05275pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#666666} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#666666} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=1.2pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 39.37 0 C 39.37 21.74 21.74 39.37 0 39.37 C -21.74 39.37 -39.37 21.74 -39.37 0 C -39.37 -21.74 -21.74 -39.37 0 -39.37 C 21.74 -39.37 39.37 -21.74 39.37 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 39.37 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -39.37 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.97 0 C 1.97 1.09 1.09 1.97 0 1.97 C -1.09 1.97 -1.97 1.09 -1.97 0 C -1.97 -1.09 -1.09 -1.97 0 -1.97 C 1.09 -1.97 1.97 -1.09 1.97 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

(73)

which is often used to represent the two-point function,

\langle O(\vec{y}_{1}),O(\vec{y}_{2})\rangle_{\mathrm{CFT}}=\int_{\mathrm{AdS}}\mathrm{d}\vec{y}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{z^{1+d}}\Big\{\partial K(\vec{y},z;\vec{y}_{1})\cdot\partial K(\vec{y},z;\vec{y}_{2})+m^{2}K(\vec{y},z;\vec{y}_{1})K(\vec{y},z,\vec{y}_{2})\Big\},

(74)

is misleading in that the ‘vertex’, which looks like it should be
\mu_{1}
, in fact is not (since we have picked up a boundary term); it can, instead, be interpreted as the AdS boundary-to-boundary propagator.

3.2
Gauge theory on a manifold-with-boundary

Here, we consider the relative
L_{\infty}
-algebras for Chern-Simons and Yang–Mills theory on oriented compact Riemannian manifolds with boundary. See also
[
32
]
for an
L_{\infty}
-algebra approach to Yang–Mills theory on a manifold with boundary .

3.2.1
Chern–Simons theory

BV action.

Consider an ordinary finite-dimensional metric Lie algebra
(V,[-,-]_{V},\langle-,-\rangle_{V})
. The naïve
18
18

18

In the sense that it is derived directly from the canonical symplectic
Q
-manifold
(\Omegaup^{\bullet}(M,V)[1],Q,\omega)
. See
[
14
]
for a detailed discussion of its
L_{\infty}
-algebra realisation.
homotopy Maurer–Cartan Chern–Simons BV action on an oriented compact Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
with boundary
\partial M
is given by

\displaystyle S^{\mathrm{CS}}_{\mathrm{hMC}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{M}\Big\{\tfrac{1}{2}\langle A,\mathrm{d}_{M}A\rangle_{V}+\tfrac{1}{3!}\langle A,[A,A]_{V}\rangle_{V}

(75)

\displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{2}\langle A^{+},\mathrm{d}_{M}c\rangle_{V}-\tfrac{1}{2}\langle c,\mathrm{d}_{M}A^{+}\rangle_{V}-\langle A^{+},[A,c]_{V}\rangle_{V}+\tfrac{1}{2}\langle c^{+},[c,c]_{V}\rangle_{V}\Big\},

where
c\in\Omegaup^{0}(M,V)
is the ghost and
A\in\Omegaup^{1}(M,V)
the Chern–Simons gauge potential and
A^{+}\in\Omegaup^{2}(M,V)
and
c^{+}\in\Omegaup^{3}(M,V)
the corresponding anti-fields. This action is invariant under the corresponding BV transformations,

\displaystyle Q_{\mathrm{BV}}c

\displaystyle\coloneqq-\tfrac{1}{2}[c,c]_{V},

(76)

\displaystyle Q_{\mathrm{BV}}A

\displaystyle\coloneqq\nabla_{M}c,

\displaystyle Q_{\mathrm{BV}}A^{+}

\displaystyle\coloneqq-F-[c,A^{+}]_{V},

\displaystyle Q_{\mathrm{BV}}c^{+}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\nabla_{M}A^{+}-[c,c^{+}]_{V},

where
\nabla_{M}\coloneqq\mathrm{d}_{M}+[A,-]_{V}
and
F\coloneqq\mathrm{d}_{M}A+\frac{1}{2}[A,A]_{V}
, up to a boundary term which is given by

Q_{\mathrm{BV}}S^{\mathrm{CS}}_{\mathrm{hMC}}=\int_{\partial M}\Big\{\langle c,\mathrm{d}_{M}A\rangle_{V}+\tfrac{1}{4}\langle[c,c]_{V},A^{+}\rangle_{V}+\tfrac{1}{4}\langle c,[A,A]_{V}\rangle_{V}\Big\}.

(77)

Note that one can integrate by parts to write (
75
) as

S^{\mathrm{CS}}_{\mathrm{hMC}}=S^{\mathrm{CS}}_{\mathrm{BV}}-\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{\partial M}\langle c,A^{+}\rangle_{V},

(78a)

where

S^{\mathrm{CS}}_{\mathrm{BV}}=\int_{M}\Big\{\tfrac{1}{2}\langle A,\mathrm{d}_{M}A\rangle_{V}+\tfrac{1}{3!}\langle A,[A,A]_{V}\rangle_{V}-\langle A^{+},\nabla_{M}c\rangle_{V}+\tfrac{1}{2}\langle c^{+},[c,c]_{V}\rangle_{V}\Big\}.

(78b)

Note also that the inner product used to define the action is only cyclic up to a boundary term,

\int_{M}\langle A_{1},\mathrm{d}_{M}A_{2}\rangle_{V}=\int_{M}\langle A_{2},\mathrm{d}_{M}A_{1}\rangle_{V}-\int_{\partial M}\langle A_{1},A_{2}\rangle_{V},

(79)

for all
A_{1,2}\in\Omegaup^{1}(M,V)
. This must be rectified by a boundary term deriving from the relative
L_{\infty}
-algebra. However, this additional term drops out of the relative homotopy Maurer–Cartan action, since
\int_{\partial M}\langle A,A\rangle_{V}=0
, so it is only visible at the level of the relative
L_{\infty}
-algebra.

Relative
L_{\infty}
-algebra.

The cochain complex of the relative
L_{\infty}
-algebra is given by
V
-valued
p
-forms on
M
and
\partial M
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overbrace{\Omegaup^{0}(M,V)}^{\in\,c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overbrace{\Omegaup^{1}(M,V)}^{\in\,A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overbrace{\Omegaup^{2}(M,V)}^{\in\,A^{+}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overbrace{\Omegaup^{3}(M,V)}^{\in\,c^{+}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underbrace{\Omegaup^{0}(\partial M,V)}_{\in\,\gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underbrace{\Omegaup^{1}(\partial M,V)}_{\in\,\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underbrace{\Omegaup^{2}(\partial M,V)}_{\in\,\alpha^{+}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\piup_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\piup_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\piup_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{1}^{\partial}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{1}^{\partial}}

(80a)

with

\begin{gathered}\mu_{1}(c)\coloneqq\mathrm{d}_{M}c,\quad\mu_{1}(A)\coloneqq\mathrm{d}_{M}A,\quad\mu_{1}(A^{+})\coloneqq\mathrm{d}_{M}A^{+},\\
\mu_{2}(c,c^{\prime})\coloneqq[c,c^{\prime}]_{V},\quad\mu_{2}(c,A)\coloneqq[c,A]_{V},\quad\mu_{2}(c,A^{+})\coloneqq[c,A^{+}]_{V},\quad\mu_{2}(c,c^{\prime+})\coloneqq[c,c^{\prime+}]_{V},\\
\mu_{2}(A,A^{\prime})\coloneqq[A,A^{\prime}]_{V},\quad\mu_{2}(A,A^{\prime+})\coloneqq[A,A^{\prime+}]_{V}.\end{gathered}

(80b)

and

\piup_{1}(c)\coloneqq c|_{\partial M},\quad\piup_{1}(A)\coloneqq A|_{\partial M},\quad\piup_{1}(A^{+})\coloneqq A^{+}|_{\partial M}

(80c)

and

\begin{gathered}\mu^{\partial}_{1}(\gamma)\coloneqq\mathrm{d}_{\partial M}\gamma,\quad\mu^{\partial}_{1}(\alpha)\coloneqq\mathrm{d}_{\partial M}\alpha,\quad\mu^{\partial}_{1}(\alpha^{+})\coloneqq\mathrm{d}_{\partial M}\alpha^{+},\\
\mu^{\partial}_{2}(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})\coloneqq[\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}]_{V},\quad\mu^{\partial}_{2}(\gamma,\alpha)\coloneqq[\gamma,\alpha]_{V},\quad\mu^{\partial}_{2}(\gamma,\alpha^{+})\coloneqq[\gamma,\alpha^{+}]_{V},\\
\mu^{\partial}_{2}(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})\coloneqq[\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}]_{V}.\end{gathered}

(80d)

In addition, we introduce the degree
-2
(before grade-shiting) bilinear form has non-vanishing components

\begin{gathered}\langle\gamma,\alpha^{+}\rangle_{\partial}\coloneqq\int_{\partial M}\langle\gamma,\alpha^{+}\rangle_{V}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\left\langle\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\right\rangle_{\partial}\coloneqq\int_{\partial M}\langle\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\rangle_{V}.\end{gathered}

(80e)

With these definitions, the relative homotopy Maurer–Cartan action (
25
) becomes

\displaystyle S^{\mathrm{CS}}_{\mathrm{rhMC}}

\displaystyle=\int_{M}\Big\{\tfrac{1}{2}\langle A,\mathrm{d}_{M}A\rangle_{V}+\tfrac{1}{3!}\langle A,[A,A]_{V}\rangle_{V}

(81)

\displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{2}\langle A^{+},\mathrm{d}_{M}c\rangle_{V}-\tfrac{1}{2}\langle c,\mathrm{d}_{M}A^{+}\rangle_{V}-\langle A^{+},[A,c]_{V}\rangle_{V}+\tfrac{1}{2}\langle c^{+},[c,c]_{V}\rangle_{V}\Big\}\Big|_{\partial M}

\displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{\partial M}\Big\{\langle c,A^{+}\rangle_{V}-\langle A,A\rangle_{V}\Big\}

\displaystyle=\int_{M}\Big\{\tfrac{1}{2}\langle A,\mathrm{d}_{M}A\rangle_{V}+\tfrac{1}{3!}\langle A,[A,A]_{V}\rangle_{V}\langle A^{+},\nabla_{M}c\rangle_{V}+\tfrac{1}{2}\langle c^{+},[c,c]_{V}\rangle_{V}\Big\}.

Relative minimal model.

To construct the relative minimal model, we first consider the cohomology of the bulk cochain complex,
(\Omegaup^{\bullet}(M,V),\mathrm{d}_{M})
. By the Künneth formula

H_{\mu_{1}}^{\bullet}(\Omegaup^{\bullet}(M,V)\cong\bigoplus_{p=0}^{3}H_{\mathrm{dR}}^{p}(M;\mathbb{R})\otimes V,

(82)

where
H_{\mathrm{dR}}^{\bullet}(M;\mathbb{R})
is the de Rham cohomology and for
M
an oriented compact Riemannian manifold with boundary
\partial M
(and possibly corners) we have
H_{\mathrm{dR}}^{\bullet}(M;\mathbb{R})\cong H^{\bullet}(M;\mathbb{R})
, the real singular cohomology of
M

[
58
]
. Thus, the minimal model absent interactions (higher
L_{\infty}
-brackets) computes the real singular cohomology of
M
.

To identify
H_{\mathrm{dR}}^{\bullet}(M;\mathbb{R})
explicitly, denote the closed, exact, co-closed, co-exact forms and their intersections by (leaving
M
implicit)

\displaystyle C^{p}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{\omega\in\Omegaup^{p}(M)\mid\mathrm{d}_{M}\omega=0\},

(83)

\displaystyle cC^{p}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{\omega\in\Omegaup^{p}(M)\mid\mathrm{d}_{M}^{\dagger}\omega=0\},

\displaystyle E^{p}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{\omega\in\Omegaup^{p}(M)\mid\omega=\mathrm{d}_{M}\eta\},

\displaystyle cE^{p}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{\omega\in\Omegaup^{p}(M)\mid\omega=\mathrm{d}_{M}^{\dagger}\eta\},

\displaystyle CcC^{p}

\displaystyle\coloneqq C^{p}\cap cC^{p},

\displaystyle EcC^{p}

\displaystyle\coloneqq E^{p}\cap cC^{p}\subseteq CcC^{p},

\displaystyle CcE^{p}

\displaystyle\coloneqq C^{p}\cap cE^{p}\subseteq CcC^{p}.

We shall also need to impose Dirichlet (relative)
D
and Neumann (absolute)
N
boundary conditions on the space of
p
-forms. To do so, it is convenient to introduce local coordinates
x=(y,r)
on
M
near
\partial M
, where
y
are local coordinates on
\partial M
and
r\geq 0
is the normal distance to the boundary so that for
p\in\partial M
we have
r(p)=0
. We denote forms at a boundary point
p
by
\omega|_{p\in\partial M}
19
19

19

Not to be confused with the restriction given by the pull-back of the inclusion, denoted by
\omega|_{\partial M}\coloneqq\iota^{*}\omega
.
, which decomposes into tangential and normal components,

\omega|_{p\in\partial M}=\omega_{p}^{\|}+\omega_{p}^{\perp}.

(84)

In local coordinates, this decomposition can be written

\omega|_{p\in\partial M}=\alpha_{p}^{\|}+\alpha_{p}^{\perp}\wedge\mathrm{d}r\quad\mbox{and}\quad\alpha_{p}^{\|},\alpha_{p}^{\perp}\in\Omegaup^{\bullet}(\partial M).

(85)

In a coordinate-free language,
\omega_{p}^{\|}
is given by
\omega_{p}^{\|}(X_{1},\ldots X_{p})\coloneqq\omega(X_{1}^{\|},\ldots X_{p}^{\|})
for all
X_{1},\ldots X_{p}\in\Gammaup(M|_{\partial M},TM)
and
X=X^{\perp}+X^{\|}
denotes the decomposition into tangential and normal parts. The normal component is then defined by
\omega_{p}^{\perp}\coloneqq\omega_{p}^{\|}-\omega|_{p\in\partial M}
. For notational clarity we will henceforth write
\omega^{\|}
and
\omega^{\perp}
for the tangential and normal components at any boundary point.

The tangential and norm components define the
D
(relative) and
N
(absolute) boundary conditions,

\Omegaup_{D}^{p}(M)\coloneqq\{\omega\in\Omegaup^{p}(M)\mid\omega^{\|}=0\}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\Omegaup_{N}^{p}(M)\coloneqq\{\omega\in\Omegaup^{p}(M)\mid\omega^{\perp}=0\},

(86)

where for (co-)exact forms the boundary conditions are applied to the pre-images

cE_{N}^{p}\coloneqq\mathrm{d}_{M}^{\dagger}\Omegaup_{N}^{p+1}(M)\quad\mbox{and}\quad E_{D}^{p}\coloneqq\mathrm{d}_{M}\Omegaup_{D}^{p-1}(M).

(87)

With these conventions, the Hodge decomposition for an oriented compact, connected, smooth Riemannian manifold with boundary is
[
59
]

\Omegaup^{p}(M)\cong cE_{N}^{p}\oplus CcC^{p}\oplus E_{D}^{p}\cong cE_{N}^{p}\oplus CcC_{N}^{p}\oplus EcC^{p}\oplus E_{D}^{p}.

(88)

Since
EcC^{p}\oplus E_{D}^{p}\cong E^{p}
, from the above,
CcC_{N}^{p}
is the orthogonal complement of the exact forms inside the closed forms,

C^{p}\cong CcC_{N}^{p}\oplus E^{p},

(89)

and we have
H_{\mathrm{dR}}^{p}(M;\mathbb{R})\cong CcC_{N}^{p}\cong H^{p}(M;\mathbb{R})
. See for example
[
60
]
. Note also that the relative
D
boundary condition applied to
CcC^{p}
gives the relative cohomology,
CcC_{D}^{p}\cong H^{p}(M,\partial M;\mathbb{R})
, cf.
[
60
]
.

Recall that when the boundary is empty,
CcC^{p}\cong\ker(\Deltaup|_{\Omegaup^{p}(M)})
by Poincaré duality and so,
\Omegaup^{p}(M)\cong CcC^{p}\oplus\Deltaup\Omegaup^{p}(M)
. Thus, the spaces of harmonic
fields
20
20

20

To use the nomenclature introduce by Kodaira in
[
61
]
.
,
CcC^{p}
, and harmonic
forms
,
\ker(\Deltaup|_{\Omegaup^{p}(M)})
, are isomorphic. However, when the boundary is non-empty there may be more harmonic
forms
than harmonic
fields
and
\Deltaup\Omegaup^{p}(M)\cong\Omegaup^{p}(M)
. See, e.g.
[
62
]
. Indeed, while
CcC_{N}^{p}
and
CcC_{D}^{p}
are finite dimensional,
CcC^{p}
and
\ker(\Deltaup|_{\Omegaup^{p}(M)})
are infinite dimensional for
0<p
.

The deformation retract data

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Omegaup^{\bullet}(M,V),\mathrm{d}_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(CcC_{N}^{\bullet},0)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{1}}

(90)

is given by the trivial embedding
i_{1}
and canonical projection
p_{1}
given by the Hodge decomposition.

We define the contracting homotopy by

h\coloneqq\mathrm{d}_{M}^{-1}P_{E},

(91)

where
P_{E}
is the projector onto the image of
\mathrm{d}_{M}
and
\mathrm{d}_{M}^{-1}
is the inverse of the operator
\mathrm{d}_{M}
restricted to the orthogonal complement of its kernel, cf. the definition of the Chern–Simons Green function in
[
63
]
. The Hodge decomposition (
88
) then implies

\mathrm{id}-h\circ\mathrm{d}_{M}-\mathrm{d}_{M}\circ h=P_{CcC_{N}^{\bullet}},

(92)

where
P_{CcC_{N}^{\bullet}}
is the projector onto
CcC_{N}^{\bullet}\subseteq\Omegaup^{\bullet}(M)
. Explicitly, using (
88
) any
p
-form can be written

\omega=\mathrm{d}_{M}\alpha+\mathrm{d}_{M}^{\dagger}\beta+\mathrm{d}_{M}\gamma+\theta,

(93)

where
\mathrm{d}_{M}\alpha\in E^{p}_{D}
,
\mathrm{d}_{M}^{\dagger}\beta\in cE^{p}_{N}
,
\gamma\in cC^{p-1}
, and
\theta\in CcC^{p}_{N}
. Consequently,

(\mathrm{d}_{M}\circ h)(\omega)=\mathrm{d}_{M}\alpha+\mathrm{d}_{M}\gamma\quad\mbox{and}\quad(h\circ\mathrm{d}_{M})(\omega)=\mathrm{d}_{M}^{\dagger}\beta

(94)

and

(\mathrm{id}-h\circ\mathrm{d}_{M}-\mathrm{d}_{M}\circ h)(\omega)=\theta,

(95)

as required.

The relative minimal model is given by
